tbmm-2013-02-14sit03spe0173par0029	Mr. President, I have made a pledge on behalf of the National Movement Party Group regarding the 1st Article of Law Proposal 410. I salute you all. Members of parliament, Turkey is not a water-rich country. We're in the climate of a semi-dry primal zone. Turkey receives an average of 642mm [642mm] per year. This rain is not too much rain, but its distribution is very different from those in the region. For example, the average rainfall in our province of Rize is about 250 millimeters [250 mm], with the average rainfall in the Central Anatolian region, such as Iconia and Karaman, dropping about 2,500 millimeters a year. Our country is heavily used for underground water. The underground water reserves are commonly used as industrial and agricultural irrigation water and continue to be heavily used. If you use these waters unplanned, I mean, if we pull more of the nutrients, the water level starts to drop. If you draw more water per year than the amount that feeds the aquifer, the water level will drop whether you want to or not. As a matter of fact, in various regions, the underwater water level was ten to fifteen years ago and is now 20 to 30 meters deep, including our provinces, Konya and Karaman. Again, in all projects that are to be done next, the closed system should be based on watering rain and drips, which means watering many areas with less water. In addition, the modernisation of irrigation networks from the dry zones should be enabled to enter the closed system. It is not possible for the government to implement this law unless such work is done. It is clear that this arrangement for our farmers in debt helix provides for their livelihood from agriculture will cause problems. May this arrangement be reconsidered, I salute the High Council.

Mr. President, valued lawmakers are being used to meet the need for high-velocity subterranean waters, the need for drinking and using water, as well as the need for agricultural irrigation of the world's rapidly increasing population. The underground water, which is socioeconomically important to our country, is also strategically important. Contamination of the groundwaters allows the surface waters to be contaminated, while pollution of the surface waters to be contaminated. Because in nature, surface water and subterranean water are directly related in many places and conditions, even if they are not directly related to each other everywhere. The goal of the proposed law, as stated in general, is to prevent levels of thought that have occurred, especially as a result of the use of dense and uncontrollably underground water in recent years, and to ensure that the underground water to be drawn or withdrawn from wells is measurable. We find the use of subterranean waters to be done within a measurement, meaningful in terms of efficient use, but we are faced with a power that compels citizens to use underground waters. Our farmers, who have to farm without irrigation channels, unfortunately have to produce agricultural water in underground waters at the cost of paying high electricity bills. A large part of the southeast suffers from the same problem, but I want to give an example of my electoral district, Mardin. There are 6,800 farmers working in the Redtepe Plain, which is known as one of the most efficient plains in Mesopotamia. The watery farmland on this plain is about 750 thousand acres. Since the GAP irrigation channels have not yet been brought to the Kızıltepe Plain, the farmers operating here pay an estimated $18,000 to $20,000 in electricity bills for water removed from the ground on every 100 acres of agricultural land. In contrast, farmers in Ceylanpınar, a neighbouring district of Kızıltepe, pay an electric bill at prices of between $800 and $1,000 for the same amount of land. Farmers in Kızıltepe are unable to pay their electricity debts at exorbitant prices, and electricity debts are increasing day by day. Even though our farmers here know the difficulty and inefficiency of using underground waters, they have to choose such a path at the cost of paying high electricity bills. Our farmers would not have suffered this kind of harm if the predicted irrigation troops had been reunited with the Kızıltepe. However, it would be unfair for our farmers to be blamed for having no alternative. What needs to be done is that the irrigation ducts are rushed to where they need to be delivered. Under the GAP Action Plan, which was launched in 2008, the watering channels that began in 2009 are not finished, and there is nothing but promises. We learned from the press three days ago by our Home Secretary, Mr. Güler, that an area of approximately 158,000 hectares would be watered by the completion of the irrigation channels needed by the Redtepe Plain. This, of course, is a good thing, but the people of the Red Hill are not waiting for good news, but for action. For years, our farmers who had to pay an electric bill for overpriced prices were no longer able to bear it, and agricultural activity in Kızıltepe has come to a standstill. Mr. President, the right and conscious use of our country's underground and aboveground waters is at the head of our duties and responsibilities. Our surface waters -- all of us know -- are being looted by small, small HESEs. Our wish is for the development of environmental-oriented policies and for the immediate fulfillment of promises made under the GAP Action Plan. Again, I salute the General Assembly, thank you.

Now we're talking about two commissions. How many deputies are there? There are 26 MPs on each commission. I see it here, and I see my MPs from my own Commission. Not just from the Republican People's Party, but two commissions make 52 MPs. The will of 52 lawmakers has been brought here without being included in this proposal. Now, Mr. Commissioner just told me: Ask the other members of the Commission about me, and they'll tell you. Now, Mr. President, I don't need to ask anyone anything. And I don't think it's necessary to have an argument with you as Commissioner, because they told you: "You will pass this law in this amount of time." They gave you instructions. So in all that time, you did what you had to do as a commissioner of the Commission who was in charge of breaking this law, and you didn't transfer it to two subcommittee commissions. It's not your fault. You, as party members, have done your duty and your instruction, but we must remind you that the 52 MPs representing the people you did not take their will to represent. Now, if you're not going to take the transfer commission reports, you're in a hurry, you're in a hurry, so answer me this: you're in a hurry, but how did you find the time to bring in the matter of publicisation that wasn't in the first bid? Everything was in order, but how did the non-proposal material get into this? Now, let's get a chapter on the so-called, citizen-related, non-finished process of two years, into a maturity -- we don't have any time -- but it's not about that at all -- about publicisation, which has nothing to do with that. Now, when it comes to that, I'd like to mention that. The representatives of the ruling party are describing: there was a case, there was a stadium, there was a stadium, there was this... but the 35th article of the Constitution is more powerful than your stadium, and more clear than your other talks. What is it? Property rights. What's the right to property? It's basic rights. What's the only exception? High public interest. The criteria for the superior public benefit are publicisation, a.k.a. the legitimacy of the domain. It's a legal requirement, a public benefit decision. So if the government is going to take possession of a citizen's property, it is possible, for the greater good of the public. Now, what you're saying is, even if this public benefit goes away, we'll sell it to private enterprises. Now, where is the individual's superior right? So when the public benefit develops, clearly, it's a clear rule of law, property rights are restored. The debate here stems from the Constitutional Court's removal of a 20-year statute of limitations on publicisation. The Constitutional Court made that decision years ago, and they entered our law. Now this is not something you can do by saying, "Let's buy the property of individuals and their rights, and we'll sell them to the third people, and we'll build apartments and build skyscrapers." Now, in particular, there's a different image being created in certain matters, but let's be clear: property rights are a constitutional right, property rights are guaranteed. You can't take back that assurance with the law. If you do, you will always be subject to compensation from the European Court of Human Rights, and I respectfully salute the General Assembly, saying that we are against the law.
